The goal of Section 7 is Corollary 7.17, which proves lack of positive recurrence in the case where there
are infinitely many strongly exposed bins. The proof in that section relies on the fact that every strongly
exposed bin has a bottleneck bin (Definition 6.10) nearby – this is Lemma 6.11, which we prove here. The
idea is that if bin j is a bottleneck bin, then the contribution to the sojourn time (between the starting empty
state and the next empty state) when balls are in bin j is ω(1), as a function of j. This is a consequence
40


## Page 41


of Lemma 6.15, which which implies a likely upper bound on the time that it takes a newborn ball to
reach bin j in a j-jammed process when bin j is a bottleneck. Before formally defining a bottleneck bin,
we give (in Definition 6.9) a couple of definitions that extend Definitions 6.1 and 6.2. Note that, since
LL(i) = Θ(log log i), it is also true that K(i) = Θ(log log i).

let K(i) = 2⌈CΥLL(i)/λ⌉.

specifying the details. Whenever we say this, we mean that i is sufficiently large with respect to λ and p.

Corollary 6.12 follows easily from Lemma 6.11 and will be useful in Section 7.
Corollary 6.12. Fix λ ∈(0, 1). Let p be a send sequence with p0 = 1 such that, for all j ≥1, Wj ≤(6/λ)j.
Suppose that there are infinitely many strongly exposed bins. Then there are infinitely many bottleneck bins.

Proof. Suppose that i is sufficiently large and is strongly exposed. Then by Lemma 6.11 there is a bottleneck
bin I with I+ = i. By Definition 6.10(iii), I ≤i ≤I exp((log I)1/2). Then if we consider another strongly
exposed bin i′ with i′ > I exp((log I)1/2) the same considerations will give another bottleneck bin I′ that
is strictly greater than I since we do not have I ≤i′ ≤I exp((log I)1/2).

7
Infinitely many strongly exposed bins
The purpose of this section is to prove Corollary 7.17 which shows that if p has infinitely many bins which
are strongly exposed then the backoff process with send sequence p is not positive recurrent. In order to get
this result we consider two auxiliary processes – the two-stream process, which is defined in Section 7.2 and
the under-backoff process, which is defined in Section 7.3. Both of these can be coupled with the backoff
process (Definition 7.6 and 7.16) so that the population of the backoff process stays above the population of
the auxiliary process. However, in each case the auxiliary process is a little bit easier to analyse.
Throughout the section we use the constants in Definition 7.1 and the events in Definition 7.2. The b
subscript stands for “bottleneck”.
Definition 7.1. Fix λ ∈(0, 1). Let cb = 1/(8 log(7/λ)) and let αb = cbλ/640.
Recall from Definition 3.6 that, given a generalised backoff process Z with send sequence p, and a set S
of bins, the expected noise at time t is N Z

Section 7.1 gives a lower bound on the probability of the events in Definition 7.2 for a j-jammed process.
Specifically, when bin i is a bottleneck and j = Θ(log(i)) Lemma 7.4 shows that with probability at least
W −1/6
i
, a pair of independent j-jammed processes both satisfy these events for i⌈exp((log i)2/3)⌉steps.
Section 7.2 (Lemma 7.9) exploits the nature of the events in Definition 7.2. Specifically, when these events
occur, it is possible to couple a two-stream process with the pair of j-jammed processes so that the two-
stream population is at least as big as the j-jammed population (intuitively, this is because the events prevent
any escapes). Hence, the probability bound from Lemma 7.4 also holds for the two-stream process, showing
that for a long period of time, both streams of the two-stream process are noisy. Section 7.3 defines the
under-backoff process (which has three streams) and shows how to couple the two-stream process with
the first two of these streams so that the two-stream population stays below the population of the relevant
streams of the under-backoff process. This guarantees (Lemma 7.12) that two of the streams of the under-
backoff process are likely to be noisy for a fairly long period. This (Lemma 7.13) makes it fairly likely that
a ball gets all the way to bin i in the third stream of the under-backoff process. This makes it easy to prove
that the expected sojourn time (from an empty state to an empty state) in the under-backoff process is an
increasing function of i (Lemma 7.14) which implies, in the presence of infinitely many bottlenecks, that
the under-backoff process is not positive recurrent. A final coupling (Definition 7.16) establishes the same
result (Corollary 7.17) for backoff.

7.2
Two-stream processes
In this section we extend the results of Lemma 7.4 to a pair of generalised backoff processes called a two-
stream process. The two-stream process was introduced in [14] as a useful device for dominating backoff
processes.
Definition 7.5. Fix λ ∈(0, 1), a send sequence p with p0 = 1, and a non-negative integer τ. A two-stream
process with birth rate λ, send sequence p and start time τ is a pair Z = (ZA, ZB) of generalised backoff
processes with send sequence p, start time τ, birth distribution D = Po(λ/2) and some initial populations
arrZA(τ) and arrZB(τ). We refer to ZA and ZB as the streams of Z. The streams evolve independently
except that eZA
j

There are no end-of-step arrivals after step τ. That is, for all D ∈{A, B}, all t > τ, and all positive
integers j, arrZD
j
= ∅.
The point of the two-stream process is that sends in one stream are only blocked by the other stream
(although randomness is used to ensure that at most one ball escapes from a given stream in a step). This
provides a little more independence than a backoff process, but it is nevertheless easy to couple a backoff
process X with a two-stream process Z so that every ball in Z is in X – the standard coupling for this is
given in Definition 7.6.

7.3
Under-backoff processes
Now we have the result that we need for two-stream processes, specifically Lemma 7.10 which says that,
with probability at least W −1/3
i
, both streams are blocked for i⌈exp((log i)2/3)⌉steps by senders (assuming
that bin i is a bottleneck and i is sufficiently large). Next, we define a final type of auxiliary process, a
so-called under-backoff process. In Lemma 7.12 we apply a coupling to lift the result of Lemma 7.10, so
that it applies to an under-backoff process. Roughly, the under-backoff has three streams and Lemma 7.12
ensures that the first two of these stay noisy for i⌈exp((log i)2/3)⌉steps so that the third stream (which is
defined slightly differently) acts as a jammed channel. This enables us, in Lemma 7.13, to show that, given
the bottleneck i, the under-backoff process has probability at least W −6/7
i
of avoiding the empty state until
a ball is in bin i of its third stream. This makes the sojourn time at least ei1/2 (Lemma 7.14). Since infinitely
many strongly exposed bins lead to infinitely many bottlenecks (Corollary 6.12), we get the final result for
under-backoff processes, Corollary 7.15, which states that under-backoff processes are not positive recurrent
when there are infinitely many strongly exposed bins. From there it will be a simple matter, in Section 7.4,
to lift this result (using one final coupling) so that it applies to backoff processes.

It is important to note that, in the under-backoff process U = (UA, UB, UC) with birth rate 3λ and
sequence p, the balls in UA and UB, taken together, are a backoff process (Definition 3.3, Definition 3.4)
with birth distribution Po(2λ), send sequence p, and cohort set C = {A, B}. On the other hand, the balls
in UC can have their escapes blocked by balls in UA and UB.

7.4
The result for backoff processes
Corollary 7.15 shows that the under-backoff process is not positive recurrent if p has infinitely many strongly
exposed bins. Specifically, the empty state is not positive recurrent, so starting there, the expected time
until the process is again empty is infinite. In order to get the same result for backoff (Corollary 7.17) we
couple the under-backoff process with the corresponding backoff process so that the population of the under-
backoff process stays under the population of the backoff process. The standard coupling, maintaining this
invariant, is given in Definition 7.16. This coupling follows the pattern of other standard couplings (for
example, Definition 7.6) though of course the details are tuned to the specific definition of the under-backoff
process.
Definition 7.16. Fix λ ∈(0, 1/3). Let p be a send sequence with p0 = 1. Let U = (UA, UB, UC) be
the under-backoff process with birth rate 3λ and send sequence p. Let X be the backoff process with birth
rate 3λ, send sequence p, and cohort set C = {A, B, C} (Definition 3.4). The standard coupling of U and
X has the following invariants for every i ≥1 and t ≥1.

sequence p is not positive recurrent.

8
High-level states for the case with finitely many strongly exposed bins
As we explained in the introduction, we analyse the backoff process by coupling it with a volume process V
that tracks sends and an infinite number of escape processes that track the escapes. The invariant is that
every ball present in the volume process V but not in an escape process is also present in the original
backoff process. Thus, to show that the backoff process is not positive recurrent, it suffices to show an
infinitely-growing population in the volume process V that is not in the escape processes.
The volume process V is designed so that, for all steps t, there is a j (depending on t) so that the
populations of the first j bins are independent Poisson variables (the volume process functions as a j-
jammed process). The dependence of the escape process on the volume process is complex, so in order to
keep things manageable, the relevant information about the state of the volume process is summarised by
the so-called “high-level state”. The high-level state is a function of the volume process, and the escape
process depends on it (but does not otherwise depend on the volume process).
A high-level state (Definition 8.1) is a tuple Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, type). At time t, the volume process
is in high-level state Ψ(t). The meaning of the elements of Ψ are as follows. j is a positive integer and
53


## Page 54


z = (z1, . . . , zj) is a tuple of non-negative reals. The idea is that when Ψ(t) = Ψ, the volume process V
acts as a j-jammed process. The non-negative integers g and τ are for book-keeping: τ is the first time such
that Ψ(τ) = Ψ, and Ψ is the g’th unique high-level state in the sequence Ψ(τ0), Ψ(τ0 + 1), . . . where τ0
is the time of the start of the volume process V . The j-tuple z has the property that, conditioned on the
transition into high-level state Ψ at time τ, dist(b
V (τ)) = Po(z). S is a set of disjoint sets of bins in [j].
The idea is that when Ψ(t) = Ψ, for all S ∈S, most bins in S provide noise in the volume process; thus
having multiple sets in S provides something stronger than a uniform bound on noise in ∪S∈SS.
Definition 8.1. A high-level state is a tuple Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, type) where g, τ ≥0 and j ≥1 are in-
tegers, z = (z1, . . . , zj) is a tuple of non-negative reals, S is a set of disjoint subsets of [j], and type
is a type in {failure, initialising, advancing, filling, refilling, stabilising}.
Given a high-level state Ψ =
(g, τ, j, z, S, type), we use gΨ to denote g, τ Ψ to denote τ, jΨ to denote j, zΨ to denote z, SΨ to denote S,
and typeΨ to denote type.
We will say more about most of the types in Definition 8.1 in Section 12, but we first define the simplest
types of high-level state – failure and initialising.
Definition 8.2. A high-level state Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, type) is a failure state if type = failure, z = 0 and
S = ∅.
To emphsise j, we refer to a failure state Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, failure) as a j-failure state. We use similar
terminology for the other types of high-level states.
Definition 8.3. A high-level state Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, type) is an initialising state if type = initialising, for
each k ∈[j], zk = 3λWk/4, S = ∅, and g = 0.
It is important that j-failure states have S = ∅. Once the volume process enters a high-level state with
type failure we expect nothing from it (we have given up) – we will show that this happens with summable
failure probability. Similarly, we don’t require any noise from a j-initialising state so S = ∅. The reason that
S = ∅is OK for the initialising high-level state is that we will set up the transition rule between high-level
states so that the volume process transitions immediately from its initialising state (and never visits it again).
The vector z of an initialising state reflects the fact that the volume process starts after the first j bins have
(mostly) filled up in expectation (for some j).
At the end of step t the volume process transitions from high-level state Ψ(t −1) to high-level state
Ψ(t) (which may or may not differ from Ψ(t −1)). The transition from Ψ(t −1) to Ψ(t) is determined
by applying a specific high-level transition rule “hls” that we will define in Section 13. The new high-level
state Ψ(t) is determined by Ψ(t) = hls(Ψ(t −1), b, t), where b is a j-tuple depending on b
V
[j](t −1) –
see definition 9.2 for details. Definition 8.4 first gives the generic definition of a high-level transition rule,
specifying some important properties which we will need, to make the coupling of the backoff process with
the volume process and the escape processes feasible.
Definition 8.4. Fix a real number λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let Ψ∗be a set of
high-level states. The domain R(Ψ∗) is the set of triples (Ψ, b, t) such that
• Ψ ∈Ψ∗, b ∈(Z≥0)jΨ, and t > τ Ψ is an integer.
• If typeΨ = initialising then t = τ Ψ + 1.
A transition rule hls on Ψ∗is a function hls: R(Ψ∗) →Ψ∗with the following properties for all tuples
(Ψ, b, t) ∈R(Ψ∗), where Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, type), b, t) and Ψ′ = (g′, τ ′, j′, z′, S′, type′) = hls(Ψ, b, t).
(T1) If Ψ′ ̸= Ψ, then g′ = g + 1 and τ ′ = t.
54


## Page 55


(T2) If type = failure, then Ψ′ = (g + 1, t, j, 0, ∅, failure).
(T3) For all S ∈S′, P
x∈S pxbx ≥λ|S|/40.
(T4) j′ ≥j. For all ℓ∈[j′] \ [j], z′
ℓ= 0. If type = initialising then j′ = j.
(T5) type′ ̸= initialising.
The definition of the domain R(Ψ, b, t) captures the fact that the transition from a high-level state Ψ
depends on Ψ. and on a j-tuple b (which for us will be the bin populations of the first j bins in the volume
process), and on time time t of the new high-level state. The second item ensures that if the initialising
state starts at time τ, then the transition out of the initialising state is at the next step, step τ + 1 (and a new
initialising state is not re-entered, by (T5)). Item (T1) is for book-keeping – it ensures that if the high-level
state changes then the time of this change is properly recorded in the new high-level state, and the “sequence
number” g is incremented.
While the volume process is in a high-level state Ψ, it evolves like a j-jammed process with j = jΨ.
The only time that the evolution of the volume process differs from the j-jammed process is when the high-
level changes to a Ψ(t) that differs from Ψ(t −1). In this case, the expectations of the new bin populations
are given by zΨ(t), which can differ from the expected populations of the jΨ(t−1)-jammed process in two
ways: first, jΨ(t) may be one larger than jΨ(t−1) (if we are moving attention from bin jΨ(t−1) to the next bin
along). Also, in order to maintain independence between bin populations while simultaneously maintaining
guarantees on dependable noise, some balls are “dropped” from the volume process when the high-level
state changes – this has to be done carefully in a way that keeps the coupling valid. Definition 8.4 puts some
restrictions on how this can be done, as follows.
(T2) guarantees that if Ψ is a failure state then so is Ψ′ (so once the volume process enters a failure state
it stays in failure states).
(T3) guarantees that for all S ∈SΨ′, P
x∈S pxbx ≥λ|S|/40. Thus, every S ∈SΨ′ acts as a guaranteed
source of expected noise for the escape process while the volume process is in state Ψ′.
(T4) guarantees that j does not decrease as high-level state changes. Also, if j increases then we do
not assume that the volume process has already acquired a population in the new bin. Finally, j does not
increase as the process leaves the initialising state.
Since the value of j can change when the high-level state changes, it will be useful to re-index the
function f (Definition 5.3) that we used to track the means of the bins of the j-jammed process. We do this
in Definition 8.5.

In practice, it is difficult to apply Definition 8.8 so what we do instead is define some properties (V1),
(V2), and (V3) and prove in Lemma 8.9 that any transition rule with these properties is valid in the sense
of Definition 8.8. When we define our specific high-level transition rule in Section 13, we will verify the
properties (V1), (V2), and (V3).
(V1) guarantees that the assumed expected starting position z′ of the next high-level state ψ′ cannot be
above the expected population sizes that would arise if the volume process just stayed in ψ. (This enables
us to keep the coupling valid – we can drop the assumed population by ignoring some balls - that only hurts
us – but we would be cheating if we created balls out of thin air.) The point of properties (V2) and (V3)
is to formalise our strategy for conditioning on sufficient noise (as captured by the high-level state) while
maintaining independent Poisson populations – it sets up conditions which guarantee this in Definition 8.8.

9
The volume process
Definition 9.2 gives the formal definition of the volume process, which we will couple with a backoff process
in order to track sends. At any point in time, the volume process mimics a j-jammed process for some j.
The value of j increases as the high-level state changes - which happens as part of the volume process,
according to the high-level state transition rule hls.
The definition is difficult to understand, so we first give a longer definition, Definition 9.1, which in-
cludes extra explanation. The explanation will be made rigorous later in Lemma 9.3 - it is just there to
aid understanding. We then give the shorter definition, Definition 9.2, which contains only the definition
(without explaining why the steps are well-defined!), and can be referred to later. The reader is encouraged
to read these in order - first Definition 9.1 with the informal explanation, then Definition 9.2 (the definition,
for future reference), and finally the lemma, Lemma 9.3. Figure 2 illustrates the definitions.

10
Escape processes
As explained in the introduction, our proof strategy is to couple the backoff process with the volume pro-
cess V from Definition 9.2 (this tracks the sends of the backoff process), and with an infinite number of
escape processes, which track escapes. A new escape process starts every time the high-level state changes.
The invariant is that every ball present in the volume process, but not in the relevant escape process, is also
present in the original backoff process. The next step is to define the notion of escape process.
Definition 10.1. Fix a send sequence p with p0 = 1, a λ ∈(0, 1), and a high-level state Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, type).
Fix a (possibly random) tuple arrE(τ) of disjoint sets arrE(τ) = (arrE
1 (τ), arrE
2 (τ), . . .) where each
arrE
i (τ) ⊆B(τ) and, for i > j, arrE
i (τ) = ∅.
An escape process E with high-level state Ψ, send sequence p, birth rate λ, and initial population
arrE(τ) is the generalised backoff process with send sequence p, start time τ, the birth distribution D that
always returns 0, initial population arrE(τ), and which satisfies the following constraints.
For all t ≥τ, define SE(t) := {S ∈S : N E
S (t + 1) ≤λ|S|/80}. Let ˆSE(t) := ∪S∈SE(t)S. Finally,
define
ΞE(t) :=
(

and νE(t) := exp(−ΞE(t)/192). For t > τ, arrE(t) and eE(t) are defined as follows.
• For each i ∈[j], arrE

different i and for different t. For all i > j, arrE

• For all i ∈[j −1] eE

Note that we have not specified the name of the ball in arrE
i (t), in the event that arrE
i (t) = 1.
Definition 10.1 needs some explanation. In the coupling that we will describe in Section 11, a new
escape process will start every time the volume process changes the high-level state. The initial population
of the escape process comes directly from the population of the previous escape process. The birth rate λ
is present as a parameter because the rate of end-of-step arrivals depends on λ, but there are no births in
an escape process. Instead, balls arrive as end-of-step arrivals - these correspond to escapes in the backoff
process. In Definition 10.1, νE(t) is defined to give an upper bound on the probability that a ball escapes
61


## Page 62


from any give bin i ∈[j] during step t of the backoff process. Its definition roughly captures the probability
of simultaneous silence from each set of bins S ∈S. The idea is that for each S, transition (T3) guarantees
that the expected noise in the volume process from balls in S is at least λ|S|/40. As long as these bins are
not too overloaded in the escape process (so that they get included in SE(t)) this expected volume noise also
corresponds to expected noise at least λ|S|/80 in the backoff process, which is accounted for in the upper
bound νE(t) on the probability of escape. If the bins in S are already too overloaded in E (captured by
N E
S (t + 1) > λ|S|/80) then these balls just contribute the trivial upper bound νE(t) ≤1. Apart from the
end-of-step arrivals, escape process is similar to a j-jammed process – this is because there is no need to
track balls after bin j.

Types of high-level states
We have already defined failure states and initialising states in Definitions 8.2 and 8.3. In Section 13 we
will define our high-level state transition rule hls. But first we define the other types of high-level states –
advancing, filling, refilling, and stabilising.

We next define j-advancing, j-filling, j-refilling, and j-stabilising states. We start by defining a j-
advancing state, which is the only one of these types that applies to a covered bin j (Definition 6.6).
Definition 12.3. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j be a positive integer. We say a
high-level state Ψ is j-advancing if bin j is covered and the following properties hold.
• typeΨ = advancing.
• jΨ = j.
• zΨ
j = 0.
• For all k ∈[j −1], zΨ
k = µγ
k.
• If bin j is many-covered then SΨ = {Υj,≥f
W[j]}. If bin j is heavy-covered then SΨ = {Υj,≥j2}.
The key to Definition 12.3 is that it applies when j is covered. As we noted in the introduction, and in
Section 6, this means that we expect bins 1, . . . , j −1 to continue providing Ω(log j) expected noise (for
the escape process) as bin j fills. The bins in the set in SΨ are the ones that we expect to provide this noise.
We will choose our high-level state transition rule so that the volume process goes to this state after all bins
in [j −1] are already reasonably full in expectation, so this is why for all k ∈[j −1], zΨ
k = µγ
k. However,
the definition sets zΨ
j = 0. This is because, when the volume process enters a j-advancing state, we don’t
66


## Page 67


assume anything about how full bin j already is – the high-level state is “advancing” to this j in order to fill
it.
As we noted, we expect at least L(j) noise while j is being filled, but some bins may go empty. There-
fore, in Definition 12.3 we define the set SΨ to be the single set S given in the definition. This choice of S
fulfils two requirements – we require both that |S| = Ω(log j) (so this set provides L(j) noise when full) and
that, subject to this, the weights of bins in S are as large as possible – either j2 in the heavy-covered case, or
at least f
W[j] in the many-covered case – so that S stays full for as long as possible. The choice of f
W[j] is
for this reason – it is is chosen in Definition 6.4 to maximise W|Υj,≥W | while ensuring |Υj,≥W | = Ω(log j).
The reason that we want to maximise W|Υj,≥W | is that, at smallest, all of the bins in Υj,≥W have weight W
so their population is Poisson with mean W|Υj,≥W | – we want this quantity to be large so that they can
maintain the desired noise for as long as possible.
The remaining types of high-level states (j-filling, j-refilling, and j-stabilising) all apply to bins j that
are exposed. In fact, they will be weakly exposed since we will ensure that j0 is chosen so that all strongly-
exposed bins are in [j0 −1]. Also (T4) ensures that j does not decrease as high-level state transitions occur.
We emphasise this point with remark 12.4.
Remark 12.4. High-level states are used in the proof of Theorem 16.1 which concerns send sequences p
with finitely-many strongly exposed bins. The proof starts by choosing a value j0 that is sufficiently large
that all strongly-exposed bins lie in [j0 −1]. The initialising high-level state Ψ0 will have jΨ0 = j0. Thus,
we will never encounter a high-level state Ψ with jΨ < j0. This means that bin jΨ is always covered, or
weakly exposed.
Definition 12.5. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j be a positive integer. We say a
high-level state Ψ is j-filling if bin j is exposed and the following properties hold.
• typeΨ = filling.
• jΨ = j.
• For all k ∈[j −1], zΨ
k = µγ
k.
• SΨ = {Υj,≥f
W[j] \ Υj,≥j2} ∪{{k}: k ∈Υj,≥jχ}.
A j-filling state, as defined in Definition 12.5 is analogous to an advancing state, but for an exposed bin.
We will show that when the volume process is in high-level state Ψ for a j-filling state Ψ, there is expected
noise at least L(j) for filling bin j.
Since bin j is exposed, we don’t expect bin j to fill without losing this noise. Hence, the high-level
state transition rule that we will use will make many high-level-state transitions (from filling to refilling to
stabilising and back to filling) as j fills. Each time the volume process enters a filling state, the bins in
[j −1] will be reasonably full in expectation, so for all k ∈[j −1], Definition 12.5 requires zΨ
k = µγ
k,
similarly to Definition 12.3. However, unlike the advancing state, it is not assumed that zΨ
j = 0. Crucially,
Definition 12.5 does not place any restrictions at all on zΨ
j . This is so that, as the volume process repeatedly
enters a filling state while bin j is filling, zΨ
j can be used to track the progress that is being made (on filling
bin j).
The set SΨ (containing sets that provide reliable noise) contains two types of set. The first type is the
set S which contains all bins in Υj,≥f
W[j] that have weight at most j2. These will typically provide expected
noise L(j) (to the escape process) for exp(Ω(j)) time, ensuring that the volume processes adds exp(Ω(j))
balls to bin j in expectation before it transitions out of a filling state.
The second type is a singleton set {k} where k is a very high-weight bin with Wk ≥jχ. There are at
least CΥLL(j)/λ such bins since bin j is weakly exposed. Like all bins in [j −1] these bins are expected
67


## Page 68


to be reasonably full when the filling state is entered, and they are useful because they will then provide at
least LL(j) expected noise for a long period (polynomial in j), even if all other bins are empty and even if
the volume process transitions to refilling and stabilising. We will use this to guarantee at least LL(j) noise
while in refilling and stabilising states, where our goal will be for the volume process to quickly refill the
bins in 1, . . . , j −1 (to re-establish noise L(j)) and re-enter a filling state.
Importantly, none of the very high-weight bins in singleton sets appear in S; thus when the noise from S
drops below L(j) and the volume process transitions out of filling, zeroing bins in S in the process, the very
high-weight bins are not zeroed. This is the reason for the factor of 2 in the denominator in (Prop 2) – we
exclude high-weight bins from S, but we must ensure that |S| still has size log j. We next give the definition
of j-refilling states.
Definition 12.6. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j be a positive integer. We say a
high-level state Ψ is j-refilling if bin j is exposed and the following properties hold.
• typeΨ = refilling.
• jΨ = j.
• For all positive integers k with Wk < j2, zΨ
k = 0.
• SΨ = {{k}: k ∈Υj,≥jχ}.
Our high-level state transition rule will have the property that the volume process goes to a refilling state
after the low-weight bins (those with weight at most j2) have become too empty, so in Definition 12.6, every
low-weight bin x has zΨ
x = 0. We keep progress on higher-weight bins, so Definition 12.6 doesn’t restrict
their z-values. Finally, the set S that maintains noise while refilling is the set of singleton sets containing
very-high-weight bins. These are the only source of noise in refilling. The purpose of refilling is to refill
bins [j −1] and we will show that it is very unlikely that these very-high-weight bins stop being full in the
time that the volume process stays in refilling, so the probability of dropping below LL(j) expected noise
while in refilling is sufficiently small. Once refilling finishes, bins [j −1] are all refilled (in expectation)
and the process will enter a stabilising state, as a means towards re-entering a filling state. There is a small
chance the transition from a refilling state into a stabilising state could “fail”. If this happens the volume
process will enter a new refilling state – we will say more about this when we define the high-level state
transition rule.
One property that the transition rule will have is that the volume process only ever enters a stabilising
state from a refilling state. The point of a stabilising state is to provide a guaranteed time window, after
leaving a refilling state, in which the low-weight bins in the first (log j)2 bins continue to provide L(j)
noise, which will be very important for analysing the relevant escape process. As far as the volume process
is concerned, the goal of the stabilising state is just for these bins to remain full for long enough (for a period
which is at most a polynomial in j). We now give the definition of j-stabilising states.
Definition 12.7. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j be a positive integer. We say a
high-level state Ψ is j-stabilising if bin j is exposed and the following properties hold.
• typeΨ = stabilising.
• jΨ = j.
• For all k ∈[j −1], zΨ
k = µγ
k.
• SΨ = {{k ∈[(log j)2]: Wk < j2}, {k ∈[j −1] \ [(log j)2]: Wk < j2}} ∪{{k}: k ∈Υj,≥jχ}.
68


## Page 69


For all k ∈[j −1], Definition 12.7 assigns zΨ
k = µγ
k (since our high-level state transition rule will
ensure that these bins start full in expectation from the refilling state). Definition 12.7 does not place any
restrictions on zΨ
j , so that progress in filling bin j can be kept.
In the definition of SΨ, we include all low-weight bins in order to provide L(j) noise for the volume
process and we also include all singleton sets containing very high-weight bins in order to provide LL(j)
noise in the event of a transition back to refilling. We split the low-weight bins into two sets – a set S1
containing those that are in the first (log j)2 bins, and a set S2 containing the others.
In order to bound the ball counts in the escape process from above, we will first use the singleton sets
in S – since there are at least LL(j) of these, it will turn out that they provide enough noise that the escape
process’s end-of-step arrival probability will be exp(−Ω(log log j)). Using this, bins in S1 will quickly
empty in the escape process. Since S1 ∈SΨ, this will drop the escape end-of-step arrival probability of the
escape process to exp(−Ω(|S1|)) which is at most a small inverse-polynomial function of j. Using this, bins
in [j −1] will then quickly empty in the escape process, and in particular bins in S2 will quickly empty; this
will ensure (with summable failure probability) that the end-of-step arrival probability of the escape process
remains low while the volume process is in the stabilising high-level state.
In Section 13 we will give the specific high-level state transition rule that we will use.
13
The backoff-bounding transition rule
We next give a high-level state transition rule (Definition 8.4) giving transitions between the specific high-
level states that we have described (failure states –Definition 8.2, initialising states – Definition 8.3, advan-
cing states – Definition 12.3, filling states – Definition 12.5, refilling states – Definition 12.6, and stabilising
states – Definition 12.7.
We will consider a positive integer j0 and a j0-initialising state Ψ0. Then the transition rule operates
on the set Ψ∗(Ψ0) (Definition 13.1) containing Ψ0 and all high-level states Ψ with jΨ ≥j0. We refer to
the high-level state transition rule hls (Definition 13.2) as the Ψ0-backoff-bounding rule – we include Ψ0 in
the name because it is the first high-level state, and we call it the “backoff-bounding rule” because it is the
high-level transition rule that we will used to bound (from below) the population of the backoff process. We
will later prove that the backoff-bounding rule hls obeys the constraints on a high-level transition rule from
Definition 8.4 (Observation 13.4) and that it is Ψ∗-valid (Definition 8.8) which we prove (Lemma 13.8) via
Lemma 8.9.
Definition 13.1. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j0 ≥2 be a positive integer. Let

j-filling, j-refilling, and j-stabilising states with j ≥j0.
The backoff-bounding rule hls is given in Definition 13.2. Recall from Definition 8.4 that R(Ψ∗(Ψ0)) is
the set of triples (Ψ, b, t) such that Ψ ∈Ψ∗(Ψ0), b ∈(Z≥0)jΨ, and t > τ Ψ. If typeΨ = initialising then t =
τ Ψ +1. Also, recall the definition of Υj,≥W from Definition 6.3 and the Definition of F from Definition 8.5.

Lemma 13.8. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p such that p0 = 1. Let j0 be a positive integer. Let

establish (V1), (V2) and (V3). These follow from Lemmas 13.5, 13.6, and 13.7.

Analysis of the volume process
The main purpose of this section is to prove Lemma 14.44 which shows that, with probability at least 99/100,
the volume process never enters a failure state, and for all j ≥j0, it quickly gets to a j-filling or j-advancing
state. Lemma 14.44 immediately implies Corollary 14.45 (which has the same content, but is expressed in

When we prove our final theorem (specifically Theorem 16.1) using the VEB coupling we will choose j0
to be sufficiently large and the volume process witll start with a j0-initialising state. The backoff bounding
rule (Definition 13.2) never decreases j in high-level state transitions, so in our analysis we will always be
able to assume that j is sufficiently large. We don’t spell out the exact requirements each time since we
will be able to freely choose j0, but the way to think about it is that j0 is sufficiently large depending on λ
and p. Specific things that we assume include j0 ≥5 and that (since these theorems only apply when p
has only finitely-many strongly exposed bins), all strongly-exposed bins lie in [j0 −1]. We also assume
that j0 −1 ≥CΥL(j0)/λ, so that we can rely on |Υj0,≥f
W[j0]| ≥CΥL(j0)/λ (see Definition 6.4). And we
assume that CΥL(j0)/λ and CΥLL(j0)/λ are at least 2, to avoid trivialities. To avoid lengthening the paper,
we won’t spell all of these assumptions each each time, we will just state in each relevant lemma that “j is
sufficiently large”.
Towards proving Lemma 14.44, we start with Section 14.1 which gives some general lemmas about
how the expected bin populations change as the volume process progresses. It introduces the key concept of
“missing” balls. In Section 14.2 we will show that the volume process is not likely to get stuck for too long
in any single high-level state. In Section 14.3 we will show that certain “bad” high-level state transitions
(including transitions to failure states) are unlikely. In Section 14.4 we will analyse the progress of the
process through the refilling-stabilising loop. Finally, in Section 14.5 we will show that the process is likely
to progress j sufficiently quickly, and we will prove Lemma 14.44.

We can also give a Corollary (Corollary 14.3) of the material in Section 5 which implicitly gives an upper
bound on how long it takes for MΨ
[j′](t) to shrink to 0, making all bins in [j′] full in expectation: this happens
in O(MΨ
[j′](t) · poly(j)) time. This upper bound will be useful for analysing the volume process when it
enters a refilling state. As can be seen from the definition of refilling state, (Definition 12.6), and from the
backoff bounding rule (Definition 13.2), when the volume process enters a j-refilling state Ψ, it only zeroes
bins of weight at most j2. Thus, MΨ
[j](t) = O(j3). In this case, Corollary 14.3 gives an effective bound on
how long it takes to repair the damage caused by entering the refilling state, and to refill the affected bins (in
expectation).

We next single out certain high-level state transitions that we will use to bound MΨ(t)
S
. From a j-filling
state (see Figure 3) the progress of the volume process that will be helpful for bounding the population of the
backoff process will be a move to a (j +1)-advancing or (j +1)-filling state (signalling that bin j has filled).
As we already explained, it is unlikely that bin j will fill without losing noise, so the backoff-bounding rule
will make many high-level-state transitions from j-filling to j-refilling to j-stabilising and back to j-filling
(following the cycle at the bottom of the figure) while bin j is filling up. Sometimes the transition from
a j-refilling state into a j-stabilising state fails - if this happens the process instead takes the self-loop to
a new j-refilling state – we refer to this self-loop as a “back transition”. The transition from j-stabilising
to j-filling could also fail, leading to a back-transition back to j-refilling (as shown by the left-pointing
horizontal arrow in the figure). Definition 14.5 gives the formal definition of these back transitions. (These
are the back-transitions of the refilling-stabilising loop.)

14.2
Quickly changing high-level state
In this section we show that the volume process is not likely to get stuck for too long in any single advancing,
refilling, or stabilising state. The reason for this is that the guard on the relevant transition out of this state is
likely to become true quickly. Filling states will be handled as part of the refilling-stabilising loop analysis in
Section 14.4 – we leave filling states until then because we expect to visit filling states multiple times in the
process of filling bin j, going through the loop each time, and losing a little progress towards establishing
the guard on (R4)(i).

14.3
Upper-bounding probability of “bad” high-level state transitions
In this section we show that certain “bad” high-level state transitions (including transitions to failure states)
are unlikely. In order to avoid repeated calculation, we start by giving the following lemmas that will be
used repeatedly.

We now proceed to show that certain high-level state transitions are unlikely. We will do this by con-

14.4
Analysis of the refilling-stabilising loop
In this section we analyse the progress of the volume process through the refilling-stabilising loop.
84


## Page 85


The main lemma in the section is Lemma 14.24 that says, starting from a transition from a j-filling high-
level state Ψ to a j-refilling high-level state, the volume process is likely to (fairly) quickly escape from the
refilling-stabilising loop, transitioning back to a j-filling high level state that has not lost many balls from
bin j (in expectation), relative to Ψ.
Recall from Definition 14.5 that back transitions are the back-transitions of the refilling-stabilising loop.
These are the self-loop transition in Figure 3 from a refilling state to another refilling state via (R5)(i) and
the left-arrow transition in the figure from a stabilising state to a refilling state via (R2)(ii) or (R6)(i).
The stopping times in Definition 14.18 will be useful in this section.
Definition 14.18. Fix a real number λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j0 be a sufficiently
large integer. Let Ψ0 be the initialising state with jΨ0 = j0 and τ Ψ0 = τ0. Let hls be the Ψ0-backoff
bounding rule from Definition 13.2. Let V be a volume process from Ψ0 with transition rule hls, send
sequence p and birth rate λ. For any t ≥τ0, let T(t) = {τ0, . . . , t} and define the following stopping times
(which could possibly have the value ∞).
trans(t) = min{t′ > t | Ψ(t′) ̸= Ψ(t)},
back(t) = min{t′ > t | Ψ(t′ −1) →Ψ(t′) is a back transition},
exit(t) = min{t′ > t | typeΨ(t′) /∈{refilling, stabilising}},
back−exit(t) = min{back(t), exit(t)}.
We start with Lemma 14.19, which says that, starting from a state in the refilling-stabilising loop without
too many missing balls, it does not take too long before the next back transition, or the next transition out of
the refilling-stabilising loop. Lemma 14.20 then gives an upper bound on the probability that this transition
is a back transition (rather than an exit from the loop).
Lemma 14.19. Fix a real number λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j0 be a sufficiently large
integer. Let Ψ0 be the initialising state with jΨ0 = j0 and τ Ψ0 = τ0. Let hls be the Ψ0-backoff bounding
rule from Definition 13.2. Let V be a volume process from Ψ0 with transition rule hls, send sequence p and
birth rate λ. For any t ≥τ0, let T(t) = {τ0, . . . , t}. Then the following statement holds for every t > τ0
and every tuple ψT(t−1) = (ψτ0, . . . , ψt−1) of high-level states such that P(ΨT(t−1) = ψT(t−1)) > 0 and
typeψt−1 ∈{refilling, stabilising}, where j = jψt−1. If ΨT(t−1) = ψT(t−1) then
(i) for all t′ in the range t −1 ≤t′ ≤back−exit(t −1), jΨ(t′) = j,
(ii) for all ˆt in the range t −1 ≤ˆt < back−exit(t −1) with typeΨ(ˆt) = stabilising, trans(ˆt) ≤ˆt + jφ+72,
(iii) If Mψt−1
[j−1](τ ψt−1) ≤2j44 then for all ˆt in the range t −1 ≤ˆt < back−exit(t −1) with typeΨ(ˆt) =
refilling, trans(ˆt) ≤ˆt + jφ+46, and
(iv) If Mψt−1
[j−1](τ ψt−1) ≤2j44 then back−exit(t −1) ≤t −1 + jφ+73.
Proof. Item (i) follows from the fact that, according to the backoff bounding rule, transitions from refilling
and stabilising states don’t change j. Also from the backoff bounding rule, there can only be one ˆt in the
range t −1 < ˆt < back−exit(t −1) such that Ψ(ˆt) ̸= Ψ(ˆt −1) – if there is such a ˆt then Ψ(t −1) =
· · · = Ψ(ˆt −1) is a j-refilling state and Ψ(ˆt) = · · · = Ψ(back−exit(t −1) −1) is a j-stabilising state.
Thus ˆt = trans(t −1) and back−exit(t) = trans(ˆt). Thus, item (iv) follows from items (ii) and (iii) since
j ≥j0 is sufficiently large so jφ+46 + jφ+72 ≤jφ+73.
Item (ii) follows immediately from Lemma 14.9. For item (iii), we’ve observed that for all ˆt in the range
t −1 ≤ˆt < back−exit(t −1) with typeΨ(ˆt) = refilling, Ψ(ˆt) = ψt−1, so the hypothesis of the lemma

Lemma 14.21 says that, after starting in a j-filling state and moving to a j-refilling state (entering the
loop), with high probability it takes only poly(j) steps before the process leaves the loop. Moreover, at most
2j distinct high-level states are entered before this happens. The point of Lemma 14.21 is that the failure
probability is very low. By Lemma 14.20, the probability that a back transition happens before exiting the
loop is exp(−Ω((log j)2)). So Lemma 14.21 shows that the probability that this happens j times in a row
is exp(−Ω(j(log j)2)) – this failure probability is small enough that we will later be able to apply a union
bound.

are not over-full can decrease in certain high-level state transitions. Lemma 14.23 then uses the bound from
Lemma 14.22 to upper bound how much these expectations can decrease over the course of the loop.

We are finally ready to prove Lemma 14.24, which says that, starting from a transition from a j-filling
high-level state Ψ to a j-refilling high-level state, the volume process is likely to (fairly) quickly escape
from the refilling-stabilising loop, transitioning back to a j-filling high level state that has not lost many
balls from bin j (in expectation), relative to Ψ.
Lemma 14.24. Fix a real number λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1. Let j0 be a sufficiently large

14.5
Likely progress towards increasing j
In this section we show that the volume process is likely to progress j sufficiently quickly. The main goal is
Lemma 14.44, which shows that, with probability at least 99/100, the volume process never enters a failure
state, and for all j ≥j0, it quickly gets to a j-filling or j-advancing state. The main parts of the proof are
Lemmas 14.42 and 14.43 which show this for a single bin – namely that from a j-filling high-level state (or
a j-advancing one in the case of Lemma 14.43) the process is very likely to reach a (j + 1)-advancing or
(j + 1)-filling state before failing, and one of these is likely to happen in poly(j)Wj steps.
Lemma 14.42 follows the progress of the volume process from a j-filling state. In order to facilitate the
proof, which is based on quite a few lemmas, Definition 14.25 captures the initial setting (so we don’t have
to keep repeating it) and defines various sets and stopping times. Some explanation of these stopping times
follows the definition.
Definition 14.25. The initial setting is defined as follows: Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1
such that, for all i ≥1, Wi ≤(4/λ)i. Let j0 be a sufficiently large positive integer where are all strongly-
exposed bins are in [j0 −1]. Fix an initialising high-level state Ψ0 with τ Ψ0 = τ0 and jΨ0 = j0. Let hls

typeψt−1 = filling, τ ψt−1 = t−1, and zψt−1

= 0 where j = jψt−1. Consider the evolution of V , conditioned
on ΨT(t−1) = ψT(t−1).

τj,full = min{t′ ≥t: typeΨ(t′) = failure or jΨ(t′) = j + 1}.
Let O0 = t −1. For all positive integers x, let
Ix = min({τj,full} ∪{t > Ox−1 : Ψ(t −1) →Ψ(t) is via (R2)(ii) from j-filling to j-refilling }).

Remark 14.26. The setting of Definition 14.25 starts at time t−1 when the volume process enters a j-filling
state. The stopping times apply from then until time τj,full when the process has either reached a failure state,
or has reached a (j + 1)-filling state or (j + 1)-advancing state. Before τj,full the process proceeds in a
very structured way (from the backoff bounding rule). We view time t −1 as the 0’th time that the process
91


## Page 92


exits the j-refilling/j-stabilising loop (into a j-filling state). After the process exits the loop for the x’th time
(unless τj,full happens), it stays in the same j-filling state (via transition (R4)(ii)) for some number of steps
and then, at time Ix, it goes via (R2)(ii) into the j-filling/j-stabilising loop for the x’th time. Unless τj,full
happens, it stays in this loop until time Ox, when it goes out via (R6)(i) into a j-filling state.
Thus, if the volume process goes into the refilling-stabilising loop at least x times while filling bin j, then
Ix is x’th time that it goes in. Ox is the x’th time that it comes out of the loop. However, these are capped
at τj,full, since from time τj,full, the volume process stops filling bin j – it either moves on to bin j + 1 or it
fails.
We will use the fact that if Ix < τj,full then Ox = exit(Ix), where exit(Ix) is the stopping time defined
in Section 14.4 – it is the first time after Ix that the high-level state is not refilling or stabilising.
For every non-negative integer x, t −1 ≤Ox ≤Ix+1 ≤Ox+1 ≤τj,full. Also, if Ox+1 < τj,full then
these inequalities are strict so Ox < Ix+1 < Ox+1. In fact, if Ox < τj,full then the definition of Ix+1 gives
Ox < Ix+1. Similarly, if Ix+1 < τj,full then Ix+1 < Ox+1.
The exact definition of A is not very important but it is important that it is a large enough polynomial
times Wj. This is adequate time for the process to be likely to fill bin j. Thus in Lemma 14.42 we will show
that it is likely that τj,full ≤t + A.
Ready is the set of steps T ′ such that the transition from Ψ(T ′) to Ψ(T ′ + 1) could potentially be via
(R4)(i), which would trigger τj,full, and hopefully move to a (j + 1)-advancing or (j + 1)-filling state. The
main point is that the value F Ψ(T ′)

backoff bounding rule. In fact, the backoff bounding rule also requires T ′ + 1 ≥τ Ψ(T ′) + j24 which may
not be true of T ′. Nevertheless, the value of F Ψ(T ′)

The stopping time tmin is the first time T ′ ∈Ready, or it is t + A if Ready = ∅(this will be low-
probability situation where we assume that the volume process may fail to reach j + 1).

is via (R4)(ii). Also, by Lemma 14.27, as long as the transition into Ψ(tmin + amin) is not via (R1) or (R2)
it will be via (R4)(i), triggering τj,full. Since tmin + j24 ≤t + A, tmin + amin ≤t + A.
LoopStarts is the set of times t′ ≤t + A that the volume process goes into the refilling-stabilising loop
while filling bin j.
Fills is the is the set of times in the interval t−1 ≤t′ < t+A such that Ψ(t′) is j-filling. From the defin-
ition of τj,full and the backoff bounding rule, there are no times t′ ≥τj,full in Fills. From Definition 14.25,
for each t′ ∈Fills there is a non-negative integer x such that Ox ≤t′ < Ix+1.
We have just seen, in Remark 14.29, that for each t′ ∈Fills, there is a positive integer x such that
Ox−1 ≤t′ < Ix. Observation 14.30 expands on this, capturing the fact that the high-level state does not
change in this period.
Observation 14.30. Consider the setting of Definition 14.28. For all positive integers x and all t′ such that
Ox−1 ≤t′ ≤Ix −1, Ψ(t′) = Ψ(Ox−1) and this is a j-filling state.
Proof. Since Ox−1 < Ix, Ox−1 is not τj,full so Ψ(Ox−1) is a j-filling state reached via (R6)(i) (or, in the
case where x = 1, it is state ψt−1). From the backoff bounding rule, any sequence of transitions from Ox−1
which repeatedly use (R4)(ii) keep the high-level state unchanged. Since Ix ≤τj,full, the backoff bounding
rule ensures that, for all t′ with Ox−1 ≤t′ ≤Ix −2, the transition Ψ(t′) →Ψ(t′ + 1) is not via (R1) (which
would trigger τj,full), (R2)(ii) (which would trigger Ix) or (R4)(i) (which would trigger τj,full), so it must be
via (R4)(ii). Thus Ψ(t′ + 1) = Ψ(Ox−1).
Observation 14.31 shows that transition (R4)(i) is not taken before a time step in Ready.

Corollary 14.38. Consider the setting of Definition 14.28 and the events of Definition 14.32. Then
P(¬E1 ∨¬E2 ∨¬E3 ∨¬E4 ∨¬E5) ≤exp(−λj/1000).
Proof. We apply a union bound to the 5 events, using Lemmas 14.33, 14.34, 14.35, 14.36, and 14.37 to get
bounds on the individual failure probabilities, and adding those failure probabilities.
We will show that, since j ≥j0 is sufficiently large, the sum is at most the bound given in the lemma
statement.
For this we use the hypothesis from the initial setting in Definition 14.25 that for all i ≥1, Wi ≤(4/λ)i.
Note that the quantity A is at most 8(4j)φWj ≤8(4j)φ(4/λ)j. In the failure probabilities in Lemmas 14.33
and 14.37, this quantity is multiplied by a quantity that is smaller than any inverse exponential function of j
so, since j is sufficiently large, these two bounds are each at most exp(−λj/900). This quantity similarly
upper bounds the failure probabilities of the other three lemmas. Thus, very crudely, adding 5 of these is at
most exp(−λj/1000).
We next give some lemmas which derive some consequences of E1 ∧E2 ∧E3 ∧E4 ∧E5. Finally, we use
these to prove Lemma 14.42, which uses the fact (from Corollary 14.38) that the events are likely, so it is
likely that these consequences hold. Also, given the consequences, the volume process transitions from a
j-filling high-level state into a (j + 1)-advancing or (j + 1)-filling state before failing, and in poly(j)Wj
steps.

Definition 14.25, which arises when the j-filling state is reached via (R3) or (R4)). The bound shows that
roughly 2λ/3 is added to Fj with each step. In fact this is how much is gained during steps when the process

loop is roughly at most Ajφ+74 exp(−λ(−(j −1)/800) and in each of these steps Fj decreases by around
at most λ.

Lemma 14.40. Consider the setting of Definition 14.28 and the events of Definition 14.32. Suppose that
E1 ∧E4 ∧E5 occurs. Then Ready is nonempty.
Proof. Suppose for contradiction that Ready = ∅. We will show that there is a t′ in the range t −1 ≤t′ ≤

In the next part of the proof we will establish that (due to the fact that E1 ∧E4 ∧E5 occurs and that
Ready = ∅, which we have assumed for contradiction) the volume process has the following predictable
behaviour, starting from O0 = t −1, and continuing until (at least) time t + A −j24. After it is in a j-filling
state Ψ(Ox), for some non-negative integer x, it stays in that high-level state for a while (for one or more
steps), and then enters the refilling-stabilising loop at Ix+1, staying in the loop for at most jφ+74 steps, and
then exits into a j-filling state Ox+1, repeating this behaviour for x + 1.
To see this, we show that E1 ∧E4 ∧E5 and the fact that Ready = ∅rules out any other transitions that
the process could take.
Start from the j-filling state Ψ(Ox). From this high-level state, E1 rules out a transition via (R1).
Transition (R2) is no problem - it just triggers Ix+1, which is part of the “predictable behaviour”. Since
we have assumed Ready = ∅, Observation 14.31 rules out a transition via (R4)(i). Transition (R4)(ii) is

15
Analysis of escape processes
The goal of this section is to prove Lemma 15.47, which says that with constant probability the escape
processes in a VEB coupling never fill up. Our approach will be as follows.
Recall from Definition 10.1 that an escape process E takes a fixed high-level state Ψ as a parameter, and
that the expected number arr(t) of end-of-step arrivals in E at time t is a function νE(t) of the vectors b
E
S (t)
with S ∈SΨ. This lack of independence would complicate the analysis substantially, so in Section 15.1 we
first introduce a “constant-escape process” whose end-of-step arrival rate ν is constant. As one might expect,
an escape process can be dominated above by a constant-escape process for as long as νE(t) ≤ν; we give
this coupling in Definition 15.2. The expected number of balls in any bin k of a constant-escape process at
time t is then a simple function of its initial state, which we denote by hk(t) in Definition 15.3. The role of h
in analysing constant-escape processes is analogous to the role of f in analysing j-jammed processes. Like
f, h is determined by a simple recurrence relation, and we end Section 15.1 by proving some useful upper
bounds on hk(t). We then extend these properties into Chernoff-based concentration bounds on bE
k (t).
Recall from Definition 11.1 that high-level state transitions in the VEB coupling are not determined by
the state of the (current) escape process – instead, the high-level states evolve together with the volume
process. For this reason, it is difficult to maintain any kind of Poisson domination of the states of the escape
104


## Page 105


process – from the point of view of any escape process, the transitions happen at unpredictable times. For
this reason, when the high-level state changes, the VEB coupling passes from one escape process to the
next, exposing the position of every ball in the initial state of the new escape process. The analysis of
the escape process has to be able to deal with these exposed states. Moreover, different high-level states
contribute different end-of-step arrival rates to the escape processes. For example, an escape process in a
filling state will gradually empty to a low level even if it is initially full, while an escape process in a refilling
state will fill rapidly to a high level even if it is initially empty. We manage this complexity with a set of
parameterised pseudorandomness conditions (invariants) which we set out in Section 15.2; we will use these
to show that while we cannot maintain a Poisson domination for escape processes, we can nevertheless
recover key desirable properties of a Poisson domination. Looked at another way, these conditions will
enable a complex induction invariant on the sequence of escape processes in the VEB coupling, where each
step of the induction will hold with summable failure probability.
In Section 15.3 we show how the invariants of Section 15.2 evolve over the course of a constant-escape
process; in Section 15.4 we apply the results of Section 15.3 to show how these invariants evolve over the
course of an escape process; in Section 15.5 we transfer the results of Section 15.4 to the context of the
VEB coupling; and finally in Section 15.6 we apply the results of Section 15.5 to prove Lemma 15.47 by
induction.
15.1
Constant-escape processes
We first define constant-escape processes and show (in Definition 15.2) how they can dominate escape
processes from above.

cess is at most stationary then this situation will persist as the process evolves. The following lemma is a
more general version of this statement, which applies even if some bins are initially above the stationary

be enough, as we should only expect a ball β in bin k ∈S to escape the process altogether by sending from

is then unlikely to ever be in a specific low-weight bin on a specific time step, so its future contribution to h
will be small. We formalise this idea in the following lemma.

We conclude the section with three concentration bounds for constant-escape processes. The first two
rely on the same application of standard Chernoff bounds, which we now state as an ancillary lemma.

15.2
Pseudorandomness properties of escape processes
We first define some constants.
Definition 15.19. Let ξ =
1
160(λ/4)107/λ3. We define P = (P0, P1, P2, . . . ) where Pk = ξ Q

Informally, we will prove the following long-term behaviour of escape processes in the VEB coupling.
Consider the high-level state Ψ = (g, τ, j, a, S, type) (where type ̸= failure) and the escape process Eg in
the VEB coupling. The “relevant” time-steps t for the study of Eg are the time-steps (starting with t = τ)
when Ψ(t) = Ψ. The high-level state transitions do not depend on the evolution of Eg, but we have do have
bounds on the timing of high-level state transitions. We will show that with summable failure probability:
• If Ψ is a j-filling or j-advancing state, νEg(t) ≤νlo
j < νhi
j for all relevant t. For all k ∈[j], bEg
k (t)
will be approximately bounded above by PkWk with error that drops over time.
• If Ψ is a j-refilling or j-stabilising state, νEg(t) ≤νhi
j for all relevant t.
• If Ψ is a j-refilling state, for all k ∈[j] with Wk suitably large, bEg
k (t) will be approximately bounded
above by PkWk with error that grows over time but remains manageable for all relevant t. Other bins
k ∈[j] may reach bEg
k (t) ≈kνlo
k Wk < j2Wk.
• If Ψ is a j-stabilising state, we will show that Eg behaves the same way as if Ψ were a j-refilling
state for a short setup period. After this setup period, we will show that Eg gradually converges to the
initial conditions that would be satisfied if Ψ were a j-filling state.
In short: if Ψ is j-filling or j-advancing, then Eg empties to a low level and remains there. If Ψ is
j-refilling, then Eg gradually fills. If Ψ is j-stabilising, then Eg fills for a short period and then empties.
Motivated by this informal description, we now define the following invariants.
Definition 15.21. Fix λ ∈(0, 1), a send sequence p with p0 = 1, an integer j ≥1, and x ∈(R≥0)j. For all
δ > 0, we define
excess(x, δ) =
j
X
k=1
max{0, xk −δPkWk}.
114


## Page 115


We can view excess as measuring the total extent to which a standard uniform bound of the form xk ≤
δPkWk fails over k ∈[j]. We will not in general be able to maintain such bounds uniformly over all bins
(due to the presence of low-weight bins), but we will be able to maintain low excess.
Definition 15.22. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) and a send sequence p with p0 = 1, and an integer j ≥1. We define the
following conditions.
(Con1) Let d ≥0 be real. A tuple m ∈Rj
≥0 satisfies (Con1) for d if excess(m, 1) ≤d.
(Con2) A tuple m ∈Rj
≥0 satisfies (Con2) if excess(m, ξ−1/3) ≤λj/500.
(Con3) Let ϖ, θ, ν ≥0 be real. A tuple m ∈Rj
≥0 satisfies (Con3) for (ϖ, θ, ν) if, for all k ∈[j] with
Wk ≥ϖ, pkmk ≤νk + (1 + θ)Pk.
(Con4) Let G be a positive real. A tuple m ∈Rj
≥0 satisfies (Con4) for G if for all k ∈[j], pkmk ≤Gj2.
Note that we do not give j as an explicit parameter in Definition 15.22, as its value is specified by the
length of m.
Definition 15.23. Fix λ ∈(0, 1), a send sequence p with p0 = 1, a positive integer j, and a tuple m ∈
(R≥0)j. For all d ≥0, we say m is a d-AF-good beginning if it has the following properties:
(AFB1) m satisfies (Con1) for d.
(AFB2) m satisfies (Con2).
For all d, ϖ, θ, G ≥0, we say m is a (d, ϖ, θ, G)-RS-good beginning if it has the following properties:
(RSB1) m satisfies (Con1) for d.
(RSB2) m satisfies (Con3) for (ϖ, θ, νhi
j ).
(RSB3) m satisfies (Con4) for G.
In general, we will require that an escape process for an Advancing or Filling high-level state has an
AF-good beginning (see Lemmas 15.44 and 15.45) and that an escape process for a Refilling or Stabilising
high-level state has an RS-good beginning (see Lemmas 15.43 and 15.46). In the remainder of this section,
we will prove some generally-useful ancillary lemmas. In Section 15.3, we will analyse the behaviour of
constant-escape processes with arrival rates νlo
j and νhi
j in terms of (Con1)–(Con4). For example, we prove
that a constant-escape process with an RS-good arrival vector remains RS-good for a short period (with
slightly increased error). In Section 15.4, we extend these results to escape processes by coupling them with
constant-escape processes. In Section 15.5, we extend these results into the context of the VEB coupling,
and in Section 15.6 we analyse the entire coupling to prove our goal of Lemma 15.47.
Lemma 15.24. Fix λ ∈(0, 1), a send sequence p with p0 = 1, and a positive integer j. Let d ≥0, and
suppose a is a d-AF-good beginning. Then a also satisfies (Con3) for (dj3, 1/(ξj3), 0), satisfies (Con4)
for 1, and is a (d, dj3, 1/(ξj3), 1)-RS-good beginning.

15.3
Analysing constant-escape processes with pseudorandom starts
Let Ψ be a high-level state, and let j = jΨ. In analysing an escape process with high-level state E, we will
be concerned with constant-escape processes with two arrival rates: νhi
j and νlo
j .
• If Ψ is an advancing or filling state, we will couple E to a single constant-escape process of rate νlo
j .
• If Ψ is a refilling state, we will couple E to a single constant-escape process of rate νhi
j .
• If Ψ is a stabilising state, our analysis will be more complicated. We will first couple E to a constant-
escape process of rate νhi
j
until its behaviour has stabilised somewhat from its initial conditions,
then couple E to a constant-escape process of rate νlo
j . We will show that with failure probab-
ility exp(−(log j)2), this second constant-escape process will reach an AF-good beginning. If it
doesn’t, we will repeat the process until success, thereby reducing the overall failure probability to
exp(−300j/λ2). After reaching an AF-good beginning, we couple to a final constant-escape process
of rate νlo
j to maintain the AF-good beginning until the next high-level state transition.
In this section, we will state and prove the results we need on these constant-escape processes. Most of these
lemmas will typically be used in multiple contexts, so we state them in a general fashion.
We now show that a constant-escape process E+ with arrival rate νlo
j and reasonable starting conditions
stabilises rapidly, with long-term ball populations bounded by bE+
k
(t) ≤PkWK (Lemma 15.28) and with
b
E+
[j] (t) acting as an AF-good beginning (Lemma 15.29).

The following lemma will be useful when we are coupling an escape process E whose high-level state
Ψ is advancing or filling to a constant-escape process E+ with arrival rate νlo
j . In order to maintain the
coupling, we will need to prove that some set in SΨ maintains low expected noise in E+. Initially, if E+
starts at an AF-good beginning, this will be guaranteed by (Con2). The following lemma will allow us to
guarantee it at all time steps.

The following two lemmas are upper bounds on how much the parameters of an RS-good beginning
can degrade as a constant-escape process E+ runs. Lemma 15.32 shows that the parameter d of (Con1)
degrades at worst linearly as time goes on, the parameters of (Con3) degrade by at most a small amount, and
the paramter of (Con4) improves over time. Lemma 15.33 shows further that if the arrival rate of E+ is νlo
j ,
then the total degradation of (Con1) (i.e. the amount d increases by as E+ runs) is bounded.

15.4
Analysing escape processes with pseudorandom starts
We first give two lemmas that bound the amount of time that an escape process can be coupled with a
constant-escape process. We will apply Lemma 15.34 to couple constant-escape processes with arrival rate

ν,τ ′ is the first time t ≥τ ′ such that νE(t) > ν. Definition 15.2
establishes useful invariants of the coupling up until T E
ν,τ ′.

We are now able to state and prove our main lemmas for analysing escape processes whose high-level
state is refilling (Lemma 15.36), advancing (Lemma 15.37) or filling (Lemma 15.37). Both proofs will be
by coupling to a single constant-escape process followed by straightforward applications of results from
Section 15.3; the only somewhat non-trivial part is making sure the couplings don’t break down. (In other
words, recalling the definition of Tν,τ from Definition 11.1, we will need to bound Tν,τ below for an ap-
propriate choice of ν and τ.) Note that we will also Lemma 15.37 in analysing escape processes whose
high-level state is stabilising, hence the more general statement.

We are now able to start analysing escape processes E whose high-level state is stabilising. Our final
lemma statement is Lemma 15.39. This proof will be more complicated than Lemmas 15.36 and 15.37, so
we break it into two parts. We first prove Lemma 15.38, which says that with non-trivial failure probability,
E will reach an AF-good beginning. Even if this doesn’t happen, Lemma 15.38 ensure that with trivial
failure probability the pseudorandomness conditions don’t degrade too much, so we can try again. The
proof of Lemma 15.39 will then be by repeated application of Lemma 15.38.
For the statement of Lemma 15.38, recall the definition of T E
ν,τ ′ from the constant-escape coupling
(Definition 15.2) – namely, for an escape process E whose start time is at most τ ′, T E
ν,τ ′ is first time t ≥τ ′
such that νE(t) > ν.
Lemma 15.38. Fix a send sequence p with p0 = 1 and finitely many strongly-exposed bins, and fix λ ∈(0, 1)
with λ sufficiently small. Let Ψ = (g, τ, j, z, S, stabilising) be a high-level state with j sufficiently large. Let

15.5
Analysing high-level state transitions in the VEB coupling
The following notation will be used throughout Section 15.5 and Section 15.6. It extends the setting of the
VEB coupling (Definition 11.1) and matches the statement of Lemma 15.47.
Definition 15.40. The VEB setting is described as follows. Fix a send sequence p with p0 = 1 and finitely
many strongly-exposed bins such that, for all i ≥1, Wi ≤(4/λ)i. Fix λ ∈(0, 1) with λ sufficiently
small. Let X be the backoff process with send sequence p, birth rate 2λ, and cohort set C = {A, B}
(Definition 3.4). Let j0 ≥5 be a sufficiently large positive integer such that all strongly-exposed bins are
in [j0 −1]. Let τ0 = T p,j0 (Definition 5.17). Let Ψ0 be the initialising high-level state with τ Ψ0 = τ0 and
jΨ0 = j0. Let hls be the Ψ0-backoff bounding rule (Definition 13.2), and let ΨBB(Ψ0) be the Ψ0-closure
of hls (Definition 8.7). Observe that hls is a transition rule by Observation 13.4 and is ΨBB(Ψ0)-valid by
Lemma 13.8.
Let Einit be the event that, for every step t′ ∈[T p,j0], nXB ≥2. Let ε be the (positive) probability that
Einit occurs. Conditioned on Einit, the VEB-coupling (Definition 11.1) couples X with a volume process
V from Ψ0 with transition rule hls, send sequence p and birth rate λ and with a sequence E1, E2, . . . of
escape processes with send sequence p and birth rate λ. The coupling uses bE0
i (τ0 + 1) as a synonym for
eX
i (τ0 + 1).
In order to analyse escape processes in the VEB setting, we will need some bounds on possible transition
times and possible sequences of high-level states. These have already been proved (with constant failure
probability) in Section 14, but we collect them here for easy reference.
Definition 15.41. In the VEB setting, we say that V is well-behaved if the following properties hold:
(WB1) For all t ≥τ0, typeΨ(t) ̸= failure.
(WB2) For all j ≥j0, there is a positive integer t ≤τ0 + (4/λ)2(j−1) such that Ψ(t) is j-filling or j-
advancing.
(WB3) For all g ≥0 with typeΨ(τg) ∈{refilling, stabilising}, there is a positive integer g′ with g + 1 ≤
g′ ≤g + 2j such that typeΨ(τg′) = filling.
(WB4) For all g ≥0 with typeΨ(τg) = refilling, τg+1 ≤τg + (jΨ(τg))φ+46.
(WB5) For all g ≥0 with typeΨ(τg) = stabilising, τg+1 ≤τg + (jΨ(τg))φ+72.
Lemma 15.42. In the VEB setting, with probability at least 98/100, V is well-behaved.

Suppose that E1 and E2 occur. We will prove that (WB1)–(WB5) occur, completing the proof. (WB1)
is part of E1. (WB2) follows from E1 and tj ≤Tj. (WB3) follows from the backoff bounding rule (Defini-

and t ∈{τ0 + 1, . . . , Tj}). (WB4) follows from item (iii) and (WB5) follows from item (ii).
We are now in a position to translate the results of Section 15.4 to the VEB setting. We will work on the
assumption that V is well-behaved (since we will be able to union bound away the failure probability). We
have one lemma for each non-trivial high-level state type: Lemma 15.43 for refilling states; Lemma 15.44
for advancing states; Lemma 15.45 for filling states; and Lemma 15.46 for stabilising states. None of these
lemmas are particularly difficult, but stating them explicitly in this section will be very convenient for the
proof of Lemma 15.47.

Main result for finitely-many strongly exposed bins
Our goal in this section is to prove Theorem 16.1, which combines the volume and escape analysis. Using
it, we then prove Theorem 4.1, which finishes our proof since we have already proved Theorem 1.2 using
Theorem 4.1.
Theorem 16.1. There exists a real number λ0 ∈(0, 1/2) such that the following holds. Consider any
λ ∈(0, λ0). Consider any send sequence p such that p0 = 1 and, for all i ≥1, Wi ≤(1/λ)i. Suppose that
p has finitely many bins which are strongly exposed. Then the backoff process with birth rate 2λ and send
sequence p is transient.

enough that all strongly-exposed bins lie in [j0 −1]. j0 should also be large enough that CΥL(j0)/λ ≥2
and CΥLL(j0)/λ ≥2.

Remark 16.2. The proof of Theorem 16.1 establishes that, with positive probability, no messages escape
before time τ0 and, for all t > τ0, P

i (t) > 0. In fact, it proves something stronger. For any t > τ0
there are two cases, depending on whether jΨ(t) is covered, or weakly exposed. If it is covered (Case 1 of the
proof), the population is actually Ω(|S|) = Ω(log(jΨ(t))). Otherwise (Case 2 of the proof), all of the very-
high weight bins in [j −1] are non-empty so the population is Ω(log log jΨ(t)). However, Corollary 14.45
establishes that for all j ≥j0, there is a positive integer t ≤τ0 +1+(8j)φ+1(Wj0 +· · ·+Wj−1) such that
Ψ(t) is j-filling or j-advancing. Also, for all i ≥1, Wi ≤(1/λ)i so jΨ(t) = Ω(log t). Thus, the population
at time t is Ω(log log log t).

Theorem 4.1. There exists a real number λ0 ∈(0, 1] such that the following holds. Consider any λ ∈
(0, λ0). Consider any send sequence p such that p0 = 1 and, for all j ≥1, Wj ≤(2/λ)j. Then the backoff
process with birth rate λ and send sequence p is not positive recurrent.

and send sequence p is not positive recurrent. If p has finitely many bins which are strongly exposed then
note that λ′′ ∈(0, λ′′

rate λ = 2λ′′ and send sequence p is not positive recurrent (in fact, it is transient).
17
Discussion
The result of this paper is Theorem 1.2 which proves Aldous’s conjecture by showing that every backoff
protocol is unstable for every positive birth rate λ, in the sense that the underlying Markov process is not
positive recurrent.
We briefly discuss three interesting directions for future work.
First, is every backoff protocol unstable in the stronger sense that the corresponding Markov process
is transient for every positive birth rate? Aldous’s proof of instability for binary exponential backoff does
establish transience. Also, the charcterisation of Kelly and MacPhee (with probability 1, only finitely many
balls escape) implies transience. The most difficult part of this paper was proving Theorem 16.1 which
shows instability for send sequences p with finitely-many strongly exposed bins. The instability result of
Theorem 16.1 is actually transience. So that is a good start. However, the corresponding result for the case
with infinitely many strongly exposed bins (Corollary 7.17) is truly a proof of lack of positive recurrence,
so that result would have to be strengthened. Moreover, the statement of Theorem 16.1 relies on several
simplifications which are enabled via reductions in Section 4. The simplification that λ is sufficiently small
(Observation 4.2) would work just as well with transience. The same holds for the simplification that p0 = 1.
However, the upper bound on bin weights relies on Lemma 4.7 which is [14, Lemma 7]. This is again a
proof of lack of positive recurrence, so it would have to be strengthened.
Kelly [19] asked whether there is any acknowledgement-based contention-resolution protocol that is
stable for any positive birth rate. Acknowledgement-based protocols are a larger class of protocols than
backoff protocols, so our result does not answer this question. Kelly proved that stability of acknowledgement-
based protocols is impossible for λ > 0.567. This was later improved by [13], who showed that there is
no stable acknowledgement-based protocol for any λ > 0.531. We agree with MacPhee [21] that there is
probably no stable acknowledgement-based protocol for any positive λ, and this is a very interesting open
question. A particularly natural family of acknowledgement-based protocols that differs from backoff is the
family of age-based protocols. An age-based protocol is associated with a send sequence p. A message
sends with probability pk when it has been in the system for k steps. The results of Kelly and MacPhee [20]
apply to age-based protocols as well as to backoff protocols. Ingenoso [17] showed instability for the case
that p is monotonically decreasing. It is possible that the method of this paper could prove instability for
every age-based protocol for every positive birth rate (though we have not considered this).
We have shown that every backoff protocol is unstable for every positive birth rate λ > 0. Now that
we know this, it would be very interesting to investigate meta-stability. How long does it take before the
channel is expected to become congested? What is the behaviour of the channel before this point? What can
we say precisely about the behaviour after that point? (For example, see Remark 16.2.)
147
